[x_{\varepsilon}]_{\sim_{\sigma}}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}_{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}_{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}_{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto[x_{\varepsilon}]_{\sim_{\rho}}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

is surjective but generally not injective.

A first regularity property of GSF is the continuity with respect
to the sharp topology, as proved in the following

Theorem 17
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
Y\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

and
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a net of smooth
functions that defines a GSF of the type
X\longrightarrow Y
. Then

(i)

\forall[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X\,\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,\forall^{0}\varepsilon:\ \sup_{y\in B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q}}(x_{\varepsilon})}\left|\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(y)\right|\leq\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-q}.

(ii)

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, the GSF
g:[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X\mapsto[\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

is locally Lipschitz in the sharp topology, i.e. each
x\in X
possesses
a sharp neighborhood
U
such that
|g(x)-g(y)|\leq L|x-y|
for all

x
,
y\in U
and some
L\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.

(iii)

Each
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(X,Y)
is continuous with respect
to the sharp topologies induced on
X
,
Y
.

(iv)

Assume that the GSF
f
is locally
Lipschitz in the Fermat topology and that its Lipschitz constants
are always finite:
L\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
f
is continuous in the Fermat
topology.

Proof.

We first prove
(i)
by contradiction, assuming
that for some
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X
and some
\alpha
there exists
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k}\downarrow 0

and a sequence
(y_{k})_{k}
of points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
|x_{\varepsilon_{k}}-y_{k}|<\rho_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{k}

but
|\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})|>\rho_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{-k}
. Define

x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=y_{k}
for
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{k}
and
x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=x_{\varepsilon}

otherwise. Then
(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(x_{\varepsilon})
but
(\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}))

is not
\rho
-moderate, which contradicts Def.
14

(ii)
.

To prove
(ii)
, we apply
(i)

to each derivative
\partial^{\alpha+e_{k}}f_{\varepsilon}
to obtain

\forall k=1,\ldots,n\,\exists q_{k}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\exists\varepsilon_{k}\in I\,\forall\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{k}]:\ \sup_{y\in B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q_{k}}}(x_{\varepsilon})}|\partial^{\alpha+e_{k}}f_{\varepsilon}(y)|\leq\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-q_{k}}.

(3.8)

Set
q:=\max_{k=1,\ldots,n}q_{k}
, so that for
y
,
z\in B_{\text{d}\rho^{q}}(x)

we get

\exists\varepsilon_{0}\,\forall\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]:\ [y_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon}]\subseteq B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q}}(x_{\varepsilon}).

(3.9)

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and
\varepsilon
small we can write

|\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}^{i}(y_{\varepsilon})-\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}^{i}(z_{\varepsilon})|=\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial^{\alpha+e_{k}}f_{\varepsilon}^{i}(\zeta_{\varepsilon})\cdot(y_{\varepsilon}^{k}-z_{\varepsilon}^{k})\right|

where
\zeta_{\varepsilon}:=y_{\varepsilon}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon})
for
some
\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
. Therefore
\zeta_{\varepsilon}\in B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q}}(x_{\varepsilon})\subseteq B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q_{k}}}(x_{\varepsilon})

and (
3.8
) implies

|\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})-\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(z_{\varepsilon})|\leq d\sqrt{n}\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-q}|y_{\varepsilon}-z_{\varepsilon}|.

Property
(iii)
follows upon setting
\alpha=0
in
(ii)
.
Property
(iv)
follows directly from the definition
of locally Lipschitz function in the Fermat topology. In fact, we
have that
L|x-y|<r\in\mathbb{R}
if
y\in B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm F}}}_{r/L}(x)
, which is an
open ball in the Fermat topology because
L\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

In the following result, we show that the dependence of the domains

\Omega_{\varepsilon}
on
\varepsilon
can be avoided since it does not lead
to a larger class of generalized functions. In spite of this possibility,
we preferred to formulate Def.
14
using
\varepsilon
-dependent
domains because the extension of
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{d})

to the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is not unique and hence introduces extrinsic
elements.

Lemma 18
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
Y\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

be arbitrary subsets of generalized points, then
f:X\longrightarrow Y

is a GSF if and only if there exists a net
v_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{d})

defining a generalized smooth map of type
X\longrightarrow Y
such
that
f=[v_{\varepsilon}(-)]|_{X}
.

Proof.

The stated condition is clearly sufficient. Conversely, assume that

f:X\longrightarrow Y
is defined by the net
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For every
\varepsilon\in I
let
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\mid d(x,\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\text{c}})>\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right\}
,

\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\mid d(x,\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\text{c}})>\rho_{\varepsilon/2}^{\frac{2}{\varepsilon}}\right\}

and choose
\chi_{\varepsilon}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
with
\text{supp}(\chi_{\varepsilon})\subseteq\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}

and
\chi_{\varepsilon}=1
in a neighborhood of
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
. Set
f_{\varepsilon}:=0

on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega{}_{\varepsilon}
and
v_{\varepsilon}:=\chi_{\varepsilon}\cdot f_{\varepsilon}
,
so that
v_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
,
then
x_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\Omega_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon

small by Thm.
10
, so
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we get
\partial^{\alpha}v_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})=\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})
.
Therefore,
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
defines a GSF of the type
X\longrightarrow Y

and clearly
f=[f_{\varepsilon}(-)]|_{X}=[v_{\varepsilon}(-)]|_{X}
.
∎

Consider a GSF
f:X\longrightarrow Y
. We want to show that for a large class
of domains
X
, the function
f
is uniquely determined by its values
on particularly well behaved points
x\in X
. These domains and these
points are introduced in the following

Definition 19
.

(i)

Let
x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then we say that
the point
x
is
near-standard
if there exists a representative

(x_{\varepsilon})
of
x
such that
\exists\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}x_{\varepsilon}=:{x^{\circ}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(
{x^{\circ}}
is called the standard part of
x
). Clearly, this limit
does not depend on the representative of
x
.

(ii)

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
{\Omega}^{\bullet}:=\left\{x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\mid\exists{x^{\circ}}\in\Omega\right\}
.

(iii)

We say that
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

contains its converging subpoints
if for all
J\subseteq_{0}I

and all
x^{\prime}\in X|_{J}
which is near standard or infinite, there
exists some
x\in X
with
x^{\prime}\subseteq x
and such that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0,\varepsilon\in J}x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}x_{\varepsilon}
.

Theorem 20
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
Y\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
and let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a GSF. If
X
contains its converging
subpoints and if
f(x)=0
for all near-standard and for all infinite
points
x\in X
, then
f=0
.

Proof.

In fact, suppose that
f
vanishes on every near-standard and every
infinite point belonging to
X
, but that
f(x)\neq 0
for some
x\in X
.
Let
(x_{\varepsilon})
be a representative of
x
. Then there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}

and
(\varepsilon_{k})_{k}\downarrow 0
such that
|f_{\varepsilon_{k}}(x_{\varepsilon_{k}})|>\rho_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{m}
,
where
(f_{\varepsilon})
is a net that defines
f
. If
\left(x_{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)_{k}

is a bounded sequence, we can extract from it a convergent subsequence

(x_{\varepsilon_{k_{l}}})_{l}
. Setting
J:=\{k_{l}\mid l\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
x^{\prime}=x|_{J}

is a subpoint of
x
and by assumption there exists some
y\in X

that satisfies
y|_{J}=x^{\prime}
and additionally is near-standard, with
the same limit as
x^{\prime}
. By construction,
f(y)\neq 0
, a contradiction.
If, on the other hand, the sequence
(x_{\varepsilon_{k}})_{k}
is unbounded,
then we can extract a subsequence with
\lim_{l\to+\infty}|x_{\varepsilon_{k_{l}}}|=+\infty
,
and can then proceed as above to construct an infinite point
y\in X

at which
f(y)\neq 0
.
∎

Analogously, we can prove the following:

Theorem 21
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
Y\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
be a net of open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

(f_{\varepsilon})
be a net of smooth functions, with
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Assume that
X
contains its subpoints. Then
(f_{\varepsilon})
defines
a GSF of the type
X\longrightarrow Y
if and only if

(i)

X\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
and
[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in Y

for all
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X
.

(ii)

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\ (\partial^{\alpha}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))\in\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{d}

for all near-standard and for all infinite points
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X
.

For example, if
\Omega
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and we define the set of compactly supported generalized points by

{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}):=\{[x_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\mid\exists K\Subset\Omega\ \forall^{0}\varepsilon:\ x_{\varepsilon}\in K\}\subseteq\langle\Omega\rangle,

then
{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega})
contains its subpoints. Internal and strongly
internal sets generated by a constant sequence
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
i.e.
[A]
and
\langle A\rangle
, provide further examples of a subset
containing its subpoints. Moreover, an arbitrary union
\bigcup_{j\in J}X_{j}

of sets, with each
X_{j}
containing its subpoints, still contains
its subpoints.

The subset
{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega})
is the natural domain for embedded distributions,
as shown in the following section.

3.0.2.
Embedding of Schwartz distributions

Introduction

Among the re-occurring themes of this work are the choices which the
solution of a given problem within our framework may depend upon.
For instance, (
3.6
) shows that the domain of a GSF depends
on the infinitesimal net
\rho
. It is also easy to show that the
trivial Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}x^{\prime}(t)-[\varepsilon^{-1}]\cdot x(t)=0\\
x(0)=1\end{cases}

has no solution in
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
if
\rho_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
.
Nevertheless, it has the unique solution
x(t)=\left[e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}t}\right]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})

if
\rho_{\varepsilon}=e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}
. Therefore, the choice of the
infinitesimal net
\rho
is closely tied to the possibility of solving
a given class of differential equations. This illustrates the dependence
of the theory on the infinitesimal net
\rho
.

Further choices concern the embedding of Schwartz distributions. Since
we need to associate a net of smooth functions
(f_{\varepsilon})
to a
given distribution
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, this embedding is naturally
built upon a regularization process. In our approach, this regularization
will depend on an infinite number
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and the choice of

b
depends on what properties we need from the embedding. For example,
if
\delta
is the (embedding of the) one-dimensional Dirac delta,
then we have the property

\delta(0)=b.

(3.10)

We can also choose the embedding so as to get the property

H(0)=\frac{1}{2},

(3.11)

where
H
is the (embedding of the) Heaviside step function. Equalities
like these are used in diverse applications (see, e.g.,
[
17
,
83
]

and references therein). In fact, we are going to construct a family
of structures of the type
(\mathcal{G},\partial,\iota)
, where
(\mathcal{G},\partial)

is a a sheaf of differential algebra and
\iota:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}

is an embedding. The particular structure we need to consider depends
on the problem we have to solve. Of course, one may be more interested
in having an intrinsic embedding of distributions. This can be done
by following the ideas of the full Colombeau algebra (see e.g.
[
49
,
45
,
44
,
50
]
).
Nevertheless, this choice decreases the simplicity of the present
approach and is incompatible with properties like (
3.10
)
and (
3.11
).

The embedding

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we use the notations
r\odot\varphi
for the function
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\frac{1}{r^{n}}\cdot\varphi\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)\in\mathbb{R}

and
x\oplus\varphi
for the function
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\varphi(y-x)\in\mathbb{R}
.
These notations highlight that
\odot
is a free action of the multiplicative
group
(\mathbb{R}_{>0},\cdot,1)
on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\oplus
is a free
action of the additive group
(\mathbb{R}_{>0},+,0)
on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We
also have the distributive property
r\odot(x\oplus\varphi)=rx\oplus r\odot\varphi
.
Our embedding procedure will ultimately rely on convolution with suitable
mollifiers. To construct these, we need some technical preparations.

Lemma 22
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
there exists
some
\mu_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
with the following properties:

(i)

\int\mu_{n}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=1
.

(ii)

\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\frac{j}{n}}\mu_{n}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=0

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

(iii)

\mu_{n}(0)=1
.

(iv)

\mu_{n}
is even.

(v)

\mu_{n}(k)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Consider the Fréchet space

F:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\mid\mu\text{ even},\ \forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}:\mu(k)=0\}

and define the continuous linear functionals
f_{m}:F\to\mathbb{R}
, where

f_{0}(\mu):=\mu(0)
,
f_{1}(\mu):=\int\mu(x)\,dx
, and
f_{m}(\mu):=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\frac{m-1}{n}}\mu(x)\,dx

(
m\geq 2
). Our objective then is to implement conditions (i)–(iii)
by showing the solvability of the system

f_{0}(\mu)=1,\ f_{1}(\mu)=1,\ f_{m}(\mu)=0\ (m\geq 2)

(3.12)

in
F
. To this end, we employ a classical result of M. Eidelheit
(
[
22
, Satz 2]
). First, the family
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is linearly
independent. Next, the topology of
F\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
is
generated by the family of norms
p_{k}(\mu)=\sup_{l+m\leq k}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(1+|x|)^{l}|\mu^{(m)}(x)|
,

k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose now that
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}
are nonzero
numbers and that the
order
of the linear functional
\sum_{m=0}^{i}\lambda_{m}f_{m}

is less or equal
l
. Here, the order of an element
f
of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

is defined to be the smallest
k
such that
|f(\mu)|\leq Cp_{k}(\mu)

for some
C>0
and all
\mu\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
i_{l}:=nl+1
,
then certainly
i\leq i_{l}
. Hence both conditions of
[
22
, Satz 2]

are satisfied and we may conclude that (
3.12
) has a solution

\mu_{n}
in
F
.
∎

Remark 23
.

In addition to conditions
(i)
-
(v)

from Lemma
22
we may require that
\mu_{n}

satisfy finitely many additional properties expressible by linearly
independent functionals as in the above proof (again by
[
22
, Satz 2]
).
In particular, we may prescribe the values for
\mu_{n}
or its derivatives
at finitely many further points.

Finally, we note that any element of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
condition
(ii)
from Lemma
22

must change sign infinitely often.

We call
Colombeau mollifier
(for a fixed dimension

n
) any function
\mu
that satisfies the properties of the previous
lemma. Concerning embeddings of Schwartz distributions, the idea is
classically to regularize distributions using a mollifier. The use
of a Colombeau mollifier allows us, on the one hand, to identify the
distribution
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)\mapsto\int f\varphi
with the GSF
f\in\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(\Omega)\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})

(thanks to property
(ii)
); on the other hand, it
allows us to explicitly calculate compositions such as
\delta\circ\delta
,

H\circ\delta
,
\delta\circ H
(see below).

It is worth noting that the condition
(ii)

of null moments is well known in the study of convergence of numerical
solutions of singular differential equations, see e.g.
[
56
,
24
,
114
]

and references therein.

Figure 3.1.
A representation of Dirac delta and
Heaviside function. A Colombeau mollifier has a representation similar
to Dirac delta (but with finite values).

Next we show that the assignment
U\mapsto{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({U}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

(
U\subseteq\Omega
open) is a fine sheaf on
\Omega
. In fact, for
V\subseteq U
,
the natural restriction map
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({U}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})\to\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({V}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

can also be written, in terms of defining nets, as
f=[f_{\varepsilon}]\mapsto[f_{\varepsilon}|_{V}]
.
Also,
{\text{\rm c}}({U})\cap{\text{\rm c}}({V})={\text{\rm c}}({U\cap V})
.

Suppose that
\Omega_{j}
(
j\in J
) is an open covering of
\Omega

and that for each
j\in J
we are given
f^{j}=[f_{\varepsilon}^{j}]\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega_{j}}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

such that
f^{j}|_{{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega_{j}\cap\Omega_{k}})}=f^{k}|_{{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega_{j}\cap\Omega_{k}})}

for all
j
,
k\in J
. Then letting
\chi_{j}
(
j\in J
) be a
partition of unity subordinate to
\Omega_{j}
(
j\in J
), the GSF
defined by the net

f_{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{j\in J}\chi_{j}\cdot f_{\varepsilon}^{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)

is the unique element of
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

with
f|_{{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega_{j}})}=f^{j}
for all
j\in J
. In particular,
we may define a corresponding notion of standard support for each

f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
by

\mbox{stsupp}(f):=\left(\bigcup\left\{\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega\mid\Omega^{\prime}\text{ open},\ f|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=0\right\}\right)^{\text{c}}.

The adjective
standard
underscores that this set is made only
of standard points; a better notion of support for GSF is defined
as
\text{supp}(f)=\overline{\left\{x\in X\mid|f(x)|>0\right\}}

and studied in
[
40
]
.

As a final preparation for the embedding of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
into
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

we need to construct suitable
n
-dimensional mollifiers from a Colombeau
mollifier
\mu
as given by Lemma
22
.
To this end, let
\omega_{n}
denote the surface area of
S^{n-1}

and set

c_{n}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\frac{2n}{\omega_{n}}&\text{for }n>1\\
1&\text{for }n=1.\end{array}\right.

Then let
\tilde{\mu}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{\mu}(x):=c_{n}\mu(|x|^{n})
.
Since
\mu
is even,
\tilde{\mu}
is smooth. Moreover, by Lemma

22
, it has unit integral and all its higher
moments
\int x^{\alpha}\tilde{\mu}(x)\,dx
vanish (
|\alpha|\geq 1
).
With this notation we have:

Lemma 24
.

Let
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\chi=1
on
\overline{B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{1}(0)}
,
and
\chi=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{2}(0)
. Also, let
b=[b_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

be an infinite positive number, i.e.
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}b_{\varepsilon}=+\infty
.
Now set

\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x):=(b_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\odot\tilde{\mu})(x)\chi(x|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|)=b_{\varepsilon}^{n}\tilde{\mu}(b_{\varepsilon}x)\chi(x|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|).

(3.13)

(i)

\forall\varepsilon:\ \mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \mbox{stsupp}(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b})\subseteq B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{2|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|^{-1}}(0)
.

(ii)

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\partial^{\alpha}\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x)|=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{N})\ (\varepsilon\to 0)
.

(iii)

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\forall q\in\mathbb{N}:\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\partial^{\alpha}(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}-b_{\varepsilon}^{n}\tilde{\mu}(b_{\varepsilon}\,.\,))(x)|=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})\ (\varepsilon\to 0)
.

(iv)

\forall q\in\mathbb{N}:\ \int\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=1+O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})\ (\varepsilon\to 0)
.

(v)

\forall q\in\mathbb{N}\,\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\ |\alpha|>0\ \Rightarrow\ \int x^{\alpha}\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})\ (\varepsilon\to 0)

.

Proof.

All the claimed properties have been proved for the special case
b_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}

in
[
19
, Sec. 3]
, and the arguments employed there carry over
in a straightforward way to the present setting.
∎

Theorem 25
.

Let
(\emptyset\not=)\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

be an open set and let
\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}
as in Lemma
24
.
Set
\Omega{}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{x\in\Omega\mid d(x,\Omega^{c})\geq\varepsilon,\ |x|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}

and fix some
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\chi=1
on
\overline{B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{1}(0)}
,

0\leq\chi\leq 1
and
\chi=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{2}(0)
.
Also, take
\kappa_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
such that
\kappa_{\varepsilon}=1

on a neighborhood
L_{\varepsilon}
of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. Then the map

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}:T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\mapsto\left[\left(\left(\kappa_{\varepsilon}\cdot T\right)*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\right)(-)\right]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}).

(3.14)

satisfies:

(i)

\iota^{b}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({-}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

is a sheaf-morphism of real vector spaces: If
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega

is another open set and
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, then
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)|_{{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega^{\prime}})}=\iota_{\Omega^{\prime}}^{b}(T|_{\Omega^{\prime}})
.

(ii)

\iota^{b}
preserves supports, hence is
in fact a sheaf-monomorphism.

(iii)

Any
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)

can naturally be considered an element of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

via
[x_{\varepsilon}]\mapsto[f(x_{\varepsilon})]
. Moreover,
\forall q\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}\ \forall x\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}):\ \left|\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(f)(x)-f(x)\right|\leq b^{-q}
.

(iv)

If
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and if
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-a}

for some
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(f)=f
. In particular,

\iota^{b}
then provides a multiplicative sheaf-monomorphism
\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(-)

\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(c(-),\mathbb{R})
.

(v)

For any
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
and any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\partial^{\alpha}T)=\partial^{\alpha}\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)
.

(vi)

Let
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-a}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Then for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
and any
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
,

\big[\int_{\Omega}\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x)\cdot\varphi(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\big]=\langle T,\varphi\rangle\quad\text{in }\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

(vii)

\iota_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{b}(\delta)(0)=c_{n}b^{n}
and
if
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-a}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then
\iota_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}(H)(0)=\frac{1}{2}
.

(viii)

The embedding
\iota^{b}
does not depend on
the particular choice of
(\kappa_{\varepsilon})
and (if
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-a}

for some
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
)
\chi
as above.

(ix)

\iota^{b}
does not depend on the
representative
(b_{\varepsilon})
of
b
employed in (
3.13
).

Proof.

We follow ideas from
[
49
, Sec. 1.2]
and
[
19
]
. Let
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)

and let
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega})
. Then there exists some
K\Subset\Omega

such that
x_{\varepsilon}\in K
for
\varepsilon
small. Also, we may assume
that
K+B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{2|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|^{-1}}(0)\subseteq L\subseteq\Omega_{\varepsilon}

for these
\varepsilon
, where
L\Subset\Omega
. Then by
(i)
of Lemma

24
, for
\varepsilon
small we have

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})=\left(\kappa_{\varepsilon}\cdot T\right)*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon})=T*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon})=\langle T,\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,)\rangle.

(3.15)

Since
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, we have a seminorm estimate of the form

\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{L}(\Omega):\ |\langle T,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\max_{|\beta|\leq m}\sup_{x\in L}|\partial^{\beta}\varphi(x)|.

Together with Lemma
24

(i)
and
(ii)
this
implies that
(\partial^{\alpha}\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))\in\mathbb{R}_{\rho}^{n}

for each
\alpha
. Hence
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}
indeed maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)

into
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.

To show
(i)
, let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
be open and
let
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in c(\Omega^{\prime})
. Then using the notations introduced
before (
3.15
), we may suppose that
L\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
,
and so for
\varepsilon
small we have
\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{\prime})
.
Therefore, (
3.15
) implies for such
\varepsilon
:

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})=\langle T,\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,)\rangle=\langle T|_{\Omega^{\prime}},\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,)\rangle=\iota_{\Omega^{\prime}}^{b}(T|_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}).

Next we show
(ii)
. Suppose first that
T|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=0

for some open subset
\Omega^{\prime}
of
\Omega
. Let
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega^{\prime}})

and pick
K\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
such that
x_{\varepsilon}\in K
for
\varepsilon
small.
As above, for
\varepsilon
small we have
\mbox{stsupp}(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,))\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
,
as well as
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})=\langle T,\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,)\rangle
,
which therefore vanishes. Hence
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=0
,
and thereby
\mbox{stsupp}(\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T))\subseteq\mbox{stsupp}(T)
.

Conversely, let
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
such that
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=0

and let
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{\prime}).
Since
(\kappa_{\varepsilon}T)*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\to T

in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, in order to show
\langle T,\varphi\rangle=0
it
suffices to demonstrate that
(\kappa_{\varepsilon}T)*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\to 0

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega^{\prime}
. Suppose
this were not the case, then we could find some
L\Subset\Omega^{\prime}
,
some
c>0
and sequences
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
and
x_{k}\in L

such that
|(\kappa_{\varepsilon}T)*\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{b}(x_{k})|\geq c
for
all
k
. Fixing any
z\in\Omega^{\prime}
and setting
x_{\varepsilon}:=x_{k}

for
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{k}
and
x_{\varepsilon}=z
otherwise then defines an element

[x_{\varepsilon}]\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega^{\prime}})
with
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)([x_{\varepsilon}])\not=0
,
a contradiction.

Consequently,
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}
induces an injective sheaf morphism
(again denoted by)
\iota^{b}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(-)\longrightarrow{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({-}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.

(iii)
: If
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)

then any derivative of
f
is uniformly (in
\varepsilon
) bounded on any

(x_{\varepsilon})
(for
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in c(\Omega)
). Thus
f\in\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.
Now let
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega})
and suppose first that
f
has
compact support. By Lemma
24

(iv)
, for any
x\in\Omega
,

f(x)=\int f(x)\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(y)\,dy+n_{\varepsilon}
, where
n_{\varepsilon}=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})

for every
q>0
. Thus for
\varepsilon
small and any
q\in\mathbb{N}
we have
by Taylor expansion

\begin{split}&(\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(f)_{\varepsilon}-f)(x_{\varepsilon})=\int(f(x_{\varepsilon}-y)-f(x_{\varepsilon}))\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(y)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}+n_{\varepsilon}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{q-1}\int\frac{1}{k!}((-y\cdot D)^{k}f)(x_{\varepsilon})\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(y)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}\\
&+\frac{b_{\varepsilon}^{-q}}{q!}\int((-y\cdot D)^{q}f)(x_{\varepsilon}-\theta_{\varepsilon}b_{\varepsilon}^{-1}y)\tilde{\mu}(y)\chi(b_{\varepsilon}^{-1}y|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}+n_{\varepsilon},\end{split}

(3.16)

where
\theta_{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
. Here, the first sum is
O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})

by Lemma
24

(v)
, as is the second term since
f

is compactly supported,
\chi
is globally bounded, and
\tilde{\mu}\in{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
If
f
is not compactly supported, pick
L\Subset\Omega
such that

x_{\varepsilon}\in L
for
\varepsilon
small and let
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
equal

1
in a neighborhood of
L
. Then
f(x)=(\varphi f)(x)
and
(ii)

implies that
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(f)(x)=\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\varphi f)(x)
,
so the general case follows as well.

(iv)
: It suffices to observe that, by our assumption
on
b
,
(iii)
implies that

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(f)([x_{\varepsilon}])=[f(x_{\varepsilon})]=f(x)
for any
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)

and any
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in{\text{\rm c}}({\Omega})
.

(v)
: As in the proof of
(iv)
we
may assume that
T
has compact support. Then for
\varepsilon
small we
have

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\partial^{\alpha}T)_{\varepsilon}=\partial^{\alpha}T*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}=\partial^{\alpha}(T*\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b})=\partial^{\alpha}\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}.

(vi)
: Pick
\zeta\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
such that
\zeta\equiv 1

on a neighborhood of
\mbox{stsupp}(\varphi)
. Then

\mbox{stsupp}(\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)-\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\zeta T))\cap\mbox{stsupp}\varphi=\mbox{stsupp}(T-\zeta T)\cap\mbox{stsupp}\varphi=\emptyset,

so we may replace
T
by
\zeta T
, i.e., we may assume without
loss of generality that
T\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(\Omega)
. By the representation
theorem of distribution theory
T
then is a finite sum of terms
of the form
\partial^{\alpha}f
with
f\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\Omega)

compactly supported in
\Omega
, so it will suffice to treat the
case
T=\partial^{\alpha}f
. For any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)

we have

\displaystyle\int(\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\partial^{\alpha}f)_{\varepsilon}-

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}f)(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\int\int(\partial^{\alpha}f(x-y)-\partial^{\alpha}f(x))\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(y)\varphi(x)\,\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}+n_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\int\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\int\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(y)(\varphi(x+y)-\varphi(x))\,\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}+n_{\varepsilon}.

with
n_{\varepsilon}=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})
for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
by Lemma
24

(v)
. As in the proof of
(iii)

it follows that also the integral term in the above equality is of
order
O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})
, giving the claim due to our assumption
on
b
.

(vii)
: The first claim is immediate from
\iota_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}(\delta)_{\varepsilon}(0)=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(0)
.
To show the second, note first that

\iota_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}(H)_{\varepsilon}(0)-\int H(y)\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(-y)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}=\int H(y)(\kappa_{\varepsilon}(y)-1)\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(-y)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}=0

for
\varepsilon
small by the support properties of
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
and

\chi
. Furthermore, since
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu(y)\,dy=1/2
, we
obtain

\begin{split}\Big|\int H(y)\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}(-y)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big|&=\Big|\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu(y)(\chi(b_{\varepsilon}^{-1}|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|)y)-1)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}\Big|\\
&\leq\int_{b_{\varepsilon}|\log(b_{\varepsilon})|^{-1}}^{\infty}|\mu(y)|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\hbox{\rm d}y}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}y$}}=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})\end{split}

for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
, so the claim follows.

(viii)
: We first note that any two choices for either

(\kappa_{\varepsilon})
or
\chi
provide sheaf morphisms as in
(i)
,

(ii)
, hence it suffices to check that the resulting
embeddings coincide on compactly supported distributions. For any
such
T
we have
\kappa_{\varepsilon}T=T
for
\varepsilon
small, so independence
from the choice of
(\kappa_{\varepsilon})
follows.

Now suppose that two different
\chi
’s have been chosen and denote
the corresponding functions from (
3.13
) by
\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}

and
\bar{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{b}
, and the resulting embeddings by
\iota^{b}

and
\bar{\iota}^{b}
, respectively. Since
T\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(\Omega)
,
it satisfies a seminorm estimate of the form

\forall\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega):\ |\langle T,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\max_{|\beta|\leq m}\sup_{x\in L}|\partial^{\beta}\varphi(x)|.

(3.17)

for some
L\Subset\Omega
. Together with Lemma
24

(iii)

this implies that, for any
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in c(\Omega)
and
\varepsilon

small, we have

|(\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}-\bar{\iota}_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon})(x_{\varepsilon})|=|\langle T,(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}-\bar{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{b})(x_{\varepsilon}-\,.\,)\rangle|=O(b_{\varepsilon}^{-q})

for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ix)
: Let
(c_{\varepsilon})
be another representative
of
b
, so
(c_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(b_{\varepsilon})
. As in the proof of

(viii)
it then suffices to show that
\iota^{b}(T)=\iota^{c}(T)

for any
T\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Omega)
. Given
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in c(\Omega)
,
let
K\Subset\Omega
be such that
x_{\varepsilon}\in K
for
\varepsilon
small.
Then by (
3.17
) and (
3.14
),

|(\iota^{b}(T)-\iota^{c}(T))(x_{\varepsilon})|\leq C\max_{|\beta|\leq m}\sup_{x\in K-L}|\partial^{\beta}(\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}-\mu_{\varepsilon}^{c})(x)|.

Inserting from (
3.13
) it follows by a straightforward estimate
that the right hand side here is of order
O(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{q})
for
any
q\in\mathbb{N}
, proving the claim.
∎

Whenever we use the notation
\iota^{b}
for an embedding, we assume
that
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies the overall assumptions of Thm.
25

and of
(iv)
in that Theorem, and that
\iota^{b}

has been defined as in (
3.14
) using a Colombeau mollifier

\mu
for the given dimension. Note in particular that by Theorem

25

(ix)
we are justified
in using the short hand notation
\iota^{b}
for the embedding defined
via any representative
(b_{\varepsilon})
of
b
.

Remark 26
.

(i)

In Def.
1
, we introduced the asymptotic gauge
\mathcal{I}(\rho)
,
and the entire construction depends on the fixed infinitesimal net

\rho
only through this set
\mathcal{I}(\rho)
. A more general
definition of asymptotic gauge is possible (see
[
44
]
). Anyhow,

[
44
, Sec. 4.3]
shows that an embedding of Schwartz’s distribution
having certain minimal properties necessarily requires that the asymptotic
gauge be generated by a single net, as is the case for
\mathcal{I}(\rho)
.

(ii)

Let
\delta
,
H\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be the corresponding
\iota^{b}
-embeddings
of the Dirac delta and of the Heaviside function. Then
\delta(x)=b\cdot\mu(b\cdot x)

and
\delta(x)=0
if
x
is near-standard and
{x^{\circ}}\neq 0
or if

x
is infinite because
\mu\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Also, by construction
of
\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}
,
\delta
can be represented like in the first
diagram of Fig.
3.1
. E.g.,
\delta(k/b)=0

for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, and each
\frac{k}{b}
is a nonzero
infinitesimal. Similar properties can be stated e.g. for
\delta^{2}(x)=b^{2}\cdot\mu(b\cdot x)^{2}
.

(iii)

Analogously, we have
H(x)=1
if
x
is near-standard and
{x^{\circ}}>0

or if
x>0
is infinite;
H(x)=0
if
x
is near-standard and
{x^{\circ}}<0

or if
x<0
is infinite.

(iv)

In
[
75
]
, S. Łojasiewicz introduced the notion of a point value
for distributions. He defined that
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
has the point
value
c\in\mathbb{C}
in
x_{0}\in\Omega
if

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle T(x_{0}+\varepsilon x),\varphi(x)\rangle=c\int\varphi(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega).

(3.18)

Not every distribution has point values in arbitrary points — in fact,
if it does, it already has to be a function of first Baire class (
[
75
]
).
Conversely, a continuous function
f
clearly has point value
f(x)

in any point
x
in its domain.

We show that if
T
has point value
c
at
x_{0}\in\Omega
then

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\to c
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. In fact,
since
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a normal space of distributions that
is invariant under translations, by
[
102
, Prop. 7]
this follows
if for any sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
, the functions
g_{k}:=\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{b}

satisfy the following conditions:

(a)

\int g_{k}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\to 1
, and
\forall\eta>0
:
\int_{|x|\geq\eta}g_{k}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\to 0

as
k\to\infty
.

(b)

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that is
1
on a neighborhood of

0
,
(1-\alpha)g_{k}\to 0
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
k\to\infty
.

(c)

For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
there exists some
M_{\alpha}>0
such
that, for any
\eta>0
:
\int_{|x|\leq\eta}|x|^{|\alpha|}|\partial^{\alpha}g_{k}(x)|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\leq M_{\alpha}
.

Indeed, all these properties follow readily from (
3.13
).

(v)

Colombeau’s special (or simplified) algebra

\mathcal{G}
(
[
15
,
16
,
83
,
49
]
) is defined, for
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

open, as the quotient
\mathcal{G}^{s}(\Omega):={\mathcal{E}}_{M}(\Omega)/\mathcal{N}^{s}(\Omega)
of
moderate
nets
modulo
negligible nets
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{M}(\Omega):=\{(u_{\varepsilon})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{I}\mid\forall K\Subset\Omega\,

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in K}|\partial^{\alpha}u_{\varepsilon}(x)|=O(\varepsilon^{-N})\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{s}(\Omega):=\{(u_{\varepsilon})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)^{I}\mid\forall K\Subset\Omega\,

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\,\forall m\in\mathbb{N}:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in K}|\partial^{\alpha}u_{\varepsilon}(x)|=O(\varepsilon^{m})\}.

It follows from
[
42
, Th. 37]
that
\mathcal{G}^{s}(\Omega)
can be identified
with the algebra
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
in the special
case of
\rho(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
. In this setting, Theorem
25

gives an alternative proof of the well known facts that the Colombeau
algebra contains
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
as a faithful subalgebra,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)

as a linear subspace and that the embedding is a sheaf morphism that
commutes with partial derivatives. An alternative point of view is
that Colombeau generalized functions correspond to those generalized
smooth functions that are defined on compactly supported generalized
points. As was already mentioned, more general domains are both useful
in applications and are indeed a necessary requirement for obtaining
a construction that is closed with respect to composition of generalized
functions.

We close this section by considering the following natural problem:
let us define two embeddings
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}
,
\iota_{\Omega}^{c}

as in (
3.14
), but using two different infinite positive
numbers
b
,
c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, so that for all
T\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\Omega)

we have

\displaystyle\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(T)

\displaystyle:=\left[T\ast\mu_{\varepsilon}^{b}\right],

\displaystyle\iota_{\Omega}^{c}(T)

\displaystyle:=\left[T\ast\mu_{\varepsilon}^{c}\right].

The following result characterizes equality of such embeddings.

Theorem 27
.

Let
b
,
c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
be infinite positive numbers and let
\mu

be a Colombeau mollifier for dimension
n
. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

be open. Then
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}=\iota_{\Omega}^{c}
if and only
if
b=c
in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e. if and only if they are equal as Robinson-Colombeau
generalized number.

Proof.

By Theorem
25

(ix)
,
\iota^{b}

is well-defined, i.e., does not depend on the representative of
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Conversely, suppose that
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}=\iota_{\Omega}^{c}

and fix any
x_{0}\in\Omega
. Then in particular
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(\delta_{x_{0}})=\iota_{\Omega}^{c}(\delta_{x_{0}})

in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
. Due to (
3.13
), (
3.14
),
an evaluation of these GSF at
x_{0}
implies

\forall m\in\mathbb{N}:\ \left|(b_{\varepsilon}^{n}-c_{\varepsilon}^{n})c_{n}\right|=O(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{m}),

so
b=c
in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

3.0.3.
Closure with respect to composition

In contrast to the case of distributions, there is no problem in considering
the composition of two GSF. This property opens new interesting possibilities,
e.g. in considering differential equations
y^{\prime}=f(y,t)
, where
y

and
f
are GSF. For instance, there is no problem in studying
y^{\prime}=\delta(y)

(see
[
76
]
).

Theorem 28
.

Subsets
S\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
with the
trace of the sharp topology, and generalized smooth maps as arrows
form a subcategory of the category of topological spaces. We will
call this category
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}
, the
category of GSF
.

Proof.

From Thm.
17

(iii)
we already
know that every GSF is continuous; we have hence to prove that these
arrows are closed with respect to identity and composition in order
to obtain a concrete subcategory of topological spaces and continuous
maps.

If
T\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{t}
is an arbitrary object, then

f_{\varepsilon}(x):=x
is the net of smooth functions that globally defines
the identity
1_{T}
on
T
. It is immediate that
1_{T}
is generalized
smooth.

To prove that arrows of
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
are closed with
respect to composition, let
S\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{s},T\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{t},R\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{r}

and
f:S\longrightarrow T
,
g:T\longrightarrow R
be GSF, then

f(x)=[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in T
and
g(y)=[g_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})]\in R

for every
x\in S
and
y\in T
, where
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{t})

and
g_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{r})
are suitable nets
of smooth functions as in Def.
14
, and where
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}

is open in
\mathbb{R}^{s}
and
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{t}
.
Of course, the idea is to consider
g_{\varepsilon}\circ f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
where
\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=f_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon})
(let us note
that, even in the case where
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
does not depend by

\varepsilon
, generally speaking
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
still depends on
\varepsilon
).

Take
x\in S
, so that
f(x)=[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in T\subseteq\langle\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\rangle

and hence
f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})\in_{\varepsilon}\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
and
x_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}

for
\varepsilon
small. If we take another representative
(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(x_{\varepsilon})

we have
f(x^{\prime})=f(x)
since
f
is well-defined and, proceeding as
before, we still have that
x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon

sufficiently small. This proves that
S\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
.
Moreover, since
[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in T
, we also have that
[g_{\varepsilon}(f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))]\in R

and
g(f(x))=[g_{\varepsilon}(f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))]
. It remains to show
that the net
(g_{\varepsilon}\circ f_{\varepsilon})
defines a GSF (Def.
14
)
of the type
S\longrightarrow R
.

To this end, let us consider any
[x_{\varepsilon}]\in S
and any
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{s}
.
We can write

\partial^{\gamma}(g_{\varepsilon}\circ f_{\varepsilon})(x_{\varepsilon})=p\left[\partial^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}),\ldots,\partial^{\alpha_{A}}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}),\partial^{\beta_{1}}g_{\varepsilon}(f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})),\ldots,\partial^{\beta_{B}}g_{\varepsilon}(f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))\right],

(3.19)

where
p
is a suitable polynomial (from the Faà di Bruno formula)
not depending on
x_{\varepsilon}
. Every term
\partial^{\alpha_{i}}f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})

and
\partial^{\beta_{j}}g_{\varepsilon}(f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))
is
\rho
-moderate
by
(ii)
of Def.
14
. Since
moderateness is preserved by polynomial operations, it follows that
also
\partial^{\gamma}(g_{\varepsilon}\circ f_{\varepsilon})(x_{\varepsilon})
is
\rho
-moderate.
∎

For instance, we can think of the Dirac delta as a map of
the form
\delta:\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and therefore
the composition
e^{\delta}
is defined in
\{x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\mid\exists z\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}:\ \delta(x)\leq\log z\}
,
which of course does not contain
x=0
but only suitable non zero
infinitesimals. On the other hand,
\delta\circ\delta:\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Moreover, from the inclusion of ordinary smooth functions (Thm.
25
)
and the closure with respect to composition, it directly follows that
every
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
is an algebra with pointwise operations
for every subset
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. For an open subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the algebra
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
contains the
space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
of Schwartz distributions.

A natural way to define a GSF is to follow the original idea of classical
authors (see
[
59
,
72
,
20
]
) to fix an infinitesimal
or infinite parameter in a suitable ordinary smooth function. We will
call this type of GSF of
Cauchy-Dirac type
; the next theorem
specifies this notion and states that GSF are of Cauchy-Dirac type
whenever the generating net
(f_{\varepsilon})
is smooth in
\varepsilon
.

Corollary 29
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be open sets and
\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(P\times X,Y)

be an ordinary smooth function. Let
p\in[P]
, and define
f_{\varepsilon}:=\varphi(p_{\varepsilon},-)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,Y)
,
then
[f_{\varepsilon}(-)]:[X]\longrightarrow[Y]
is a GSF. In particular,
if
f:[X]\longrightarrow[Y]
is a GSF defined by
(f_{\varepsilon})
and
the net
(f_{\varepsilon})
is smooth in
\varepsilon
, i.e. if

\exists\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}((0,1)\times X,Y):\ f_{\varepsilon}=\varphi(\varepsilon,-)\quad\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1),

and if
[\varepsilon]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, then the GSF
f
is of Cauchy-Dirac type
because
f(x)=\varphi([\varepsilon],x)
for all
x\in[X]
. Finally, Cauchy-Dirac
GSF are closed with respect to composition.

Proof.

In fact, the map
x\in[X]\mapsto(p,x)\in[P]\times[X]
is trivially
generalized smooth and hence from the inclusion of smooth functions
(Theorem
25
) and the closure with respect to composition
(Theorem
28
) the conclusions follow.
∎

Example 30
.

The composition
\delta\circ\delta\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

is given by
(\delta\circ\delta)(x)=b\mu\left(b^{2}\mu(bx)\right)

and is an even function. If
x
is near-standard and
{x^{\circ}}\neq 0
,
or
x
is infinite, then
(\delta\circ\delta)(x)=b
. Since
(\delta\circ\delta)(0)=0
,
by the intermediate value theorem (see Cor.
48

below), we have that
\delta\circ\delta
attains any value in the
interval
[0,b]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. If
0\leq x\leq\frac{1}{2b}
, then
(for a
\mu
as in Fig.
3.1
)
x
is infinitesimal
and
(\delta\circ\delta)(x)=0
because
\delta(x)\geq b\mu\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)

is an infinite number. If
x=\frac{k}{b}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
then
x
is still infinitesimal but
(\delta\circ\delta)(x)=b
because

\mu(bx)=0
. A representation of
\delta\circ\delta
is given in
Fig.
3.2
. Analogously, one can deal with
H\circ\delta

and
\delta\circ H
.

Figure 3.2.
A representation of
\delta\circ\delta

The theory of GSF originates from the theory of Colombeau quotient
algebras. In this well-developed approach, strong analytic tools,
including microlocal analysis, and an elaborate theory of pseudodifferential
and Fourier integral operators have been developed over the past few
years (cf.
[
15
,
16
,
83
,
49
,
53
,
31
]
and references therein).
In these quotient algebras, each generalized function generates a
unique GSF defined on a subset of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$(\varepsilon)$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$(\varepsilon)$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$(\varepsilon)$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$(\varepsilon)$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. On the other hand,
Colombeau generalized functions are in general not closed with respect
to composition because they cannot be defined on arbitrary domains

X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We refer to
[
42
]
for details about
the links between Colombeau algebras and GSF, and to
[
111
,
112
,
113
]

for a treatment of Colombeau algebras in the framework of nonstandard
analysis.

4.
Differential calculus and the Fermat-Reyes
theorem

In this section we show how the derivatives of a GSF can be calculated
using a form of incremental ratio. The idea is to prove the Fermat-Reyes
theorem for GSF (see
[
37
,
39
,
61
]
). Essentially, this
theorem shows the existence and uniqueness of another GSF serving
as incremental ratio. This is the first of a long list of results
demonstrating the close similarities between ordinary smooth functions
and GSF.

We recall that the
thickening
of an open set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

along

v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\text{\rm th}_{v}(\Omega):=\{(x,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mid[x,x+hv]_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\subseteq\Omega\}
,
and serves as a natural domain of a partial incremental ratio along

v
of any function defined on
\Omega
. In order to prove the Fermat-Reyes
theorem, it is simpler to define what
a
thickening of
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

along
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is.

Definition 31
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
then we say that
T\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
is
a (sharp) thickening

of
U
along
v
if

(i)

\forall x\in U:\ (x,0)\in T

(ii)

For all
(x,h)\in T
there exist

a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, with
b<a
, such that:

(a)

|h\cdot v|<b

(b)

B_{a}(x)\subseteq U

(c)

B_{a}(x)\times B_{b}(0)\subseteq T
.

Finally, we will say that
T
is
a

large thickening
of
U
along
v
if the radii
a
,
b
in
(ii)

are real:
a
,
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Remark 32
.

(i)

Conditions
(i)
and
(ii)

imply that necessarily
U
is a sharply open set, whereas
U
is
a large open set if
T
is a large thickening.

(ii)

Let
(x,h)\in T
and let the radii

a
,
b
be as in
(ii)
, then for all
s\in[0,1]

we have
|x+shv-x|\leq|hv|<b<a
. Therefore
[x,x+hv]\subseteq B_{a}(x)\subseteq U
.
This gives a connection with the classical definition of thickening
and shows that if
f:U\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, we can consider the difference

f(x+hv)-f(x)
.

(iii)

Condition
(ii)
of Def.
31

yields that
T
is a sharply open subset of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}{}^{n+1}
; it
is a large open subset in case
T
is a large thickening.

(iv)

If
T
and
\bar{T}
are two (large) thickenings of
U
along
v
,
then also
T\cap\bar{T}
is a (large) thickening of the same type.
Finally, thickenings are also closed with respect to arbitrary non
empty unions.

In the present setting, the Fermat-Reyes theorem is the
following.

Theorem 33
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
sharply open set, let
v=[v_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

be a generalized smooth map generated by the net of smooth functions

f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R})
. Then

(i)

If
S
is a thickening of U along
v

such that
S\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
, then
there exists a thickening
T\subseteq S
of
U
along
v
and a
generalized smooth map
r\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(T,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, called the

generalized incremental ratio
of
f

along

v
, such
that

f(x+hv)=f(x)+h\cdot r(x,h)\qquad\forall(x,h)\in T.

Moreover
r(x,0)=\left[\frac{\partial f_{\varepsilon}}{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon})\right]

for every
x\in U
, and we can thus define
\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}(x):=r(x,0)
,
so that
\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.

(ii)

Any two generalized incremental ratios
of
f
coincide on the intersection of their domains.

If
U
is a large open set and
S
is a large thickening
of
U
along
v
, then an analogous statement holds for a large
thickening
T
of
U
along
v
.

Note that this result allows us to consider the partial derivative
of
f
with respect to an arbitrary generalized vector
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

which can be, e.g., near-standard or infinite.

Before proving the theorem, it is essential to show that
GSF are uniquely determined by invertible elements.

Lemma 34
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open
set in the sharp topology, and let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d})

be a GSF. Then
f(x)=0
for every
x\in U
if and only if
f(x)=0

for all
x\in U
such that
|x|
is invertible.

Proof.

Using Lem.
8
, it is straightforward to prove that
the group of invertible elements is dense in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
with respect
to the sharp topology. This implies that the set of points in
U

whose every coordinate is invertible is dense in
U
. Clearly for
any such point
y
,
|y|
is invertible. Thus given any point
x\in U

there exists a sequence
(x_{k})
in
U
converging to
x
in the
sharp topology and such that
|x_{k}|
is invertible for each
k
.
Since
f
is continuous with respect to the sharp topology (Thm.
17

(iii)
), this yields
0=f(x_{k})\to f(x)
.
∎

To show the existence of thickenings, we also need the following
result

Lemma 35
.

Let
(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
be a net of open
sets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
v=[v_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then

(i)

if
x\in\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
then
(x,0)\in\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.

(ii)

If
(x,h)\in\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle

then
x+thv\in\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

(iii)

If
U\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle

is sharply open, there exists a sharp thickening
T
of
U
along

v
such that
T\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.

The same properties hold if we consider the strongly internal
sets
\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm F}}
and
\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm F}}

in the Fermat topology. In this case in
(iii)
,

U
has to be supposed large open and the resulting thickening is
large as well.

Proof.

If
x\in\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
, then
x_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
for

\varepsilon
small, and we also have
(x_{\varepsilon},0)\in\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})

for the same
\varepsilon
. Now take
(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(x_{\varepsilon},0)
,
so that
x=[x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}]\in\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
and hence
B_{r}(x)\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle

for some
r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
r_{\varepsilon}<d(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{c})

for all
\varepsilon\in I
(see Thm.
10
).
The net
(z_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}0
, so we also have
|z_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}|<r_{\varepsilon}

for
\varepsilon
small. Thus for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small we obtain both

x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and
|z_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}|<r_{\varepsilon}
, so
that for all
s\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
we have that
|x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+sz_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}-x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}|\leq|z_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}|<r_{\varepsilon}<d(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{c})
.
Hence
x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+sz_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.
