(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon})\in\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})

for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. This shows that
(x,0)\in\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
,
implying
(i)
.

To prove
(ii)
, assume that
(x,h)\in\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle

and
t\in[0,1]
. Therefore,
0\leq t_{\varepsilon}\leq 1
for
\varepsilon
small
and some representative
(t_{\varepsilon})
of
t
. Since
(x_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})\in_{\varepsilon}\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega{}_{\varepsilon})
,
we have that
x_{\varepsilon}+t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}

for
\varepsilon
small. If we take another representative
(y_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(x_{\varepsilon}+t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})
,
then we can define
x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=y_{\varepsilon}-t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}

so that
(x_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})\sim_{\rho}(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})
. From

(x_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})\in_{\varepsilon}\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
we
thus get that also
(x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})\in\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})

for
\varepsilon
small. Therefore
x^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}=y_{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}

for
\varepsilon
small. This shows that
x+thv\in\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
.

Finally, in order to prove
(iii)
, we assume
that
U\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
is a sharply open subset. For
all
x\in U\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
, we have
(x,0)\in\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle

from
(i)
, and hence Thm.
10

(iv)
yields the existence of
c_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

such that
B_{c_{x}}(x,0)\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.
Since
U
is a neighborhood of
x
, there exists
a_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,

a_{x}<c_{x}
, such that
B_{a_{x}}(x)\subseteq U
. Choose
b_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

such that
b_{x}<a_{x}
and
a_{x}+b_{x}<c_{x}
. Because
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

is
\rho
-moderate, we have
|v|<\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Take
d_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
d_{x}<b_{x}\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{N}

and define

T:=\bigcup_{x\in U}B_{a_{x}}(x)\times B_{d_{x}}(0).

If
|h|<d_{x}
, then
|h\cdot v|<|v|\cdot d_{x}<b_{x}
and hence

T
is a sharp thickening of
U
along
v
. We finally note that

(x^{\prime},h)\in B_{a_{x}}(x)\times B_{d_{x}}(0)
implies
|(x^{\prime},h)-(x,0)|\leq|x^{\prime}-x|+|h|<a_{x}+d_{x}<a_{x}+b_{x}<c_{x}
,
so that
(x^{\prime},h)\in B_{c_{x}}(x,0)\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.
Therefore
T\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.

Considering
\sim_{\text{F}}
instead of
\sim_{\rho}
and radii
in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, in the same way we can prove the analogous properties
for strongly internal sets in the Fermat topology.
∎

We can now prove the Fermat-Reyes theorem for GSF.

Proof of Theorem
33
.

Since
U
is sharply open, for any point
x\in U
we can find a
ball
B_{R_{x}}(x)\subseteq U
,
R_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. Define
a_{x}:=\frac{R_{x}}{2}

and
b_{x}:=\frac{a_{x}}{2}
. Because
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is
\rho
-moderate,
we have
|v|<\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Take
d_{x}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

such that
d_{x}<b_{x}\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{N}
and set
T:=\bigcup_{x\in U}B_{a_{x}}(x)\times B_{d_{x}}(0)
.
Since for all
x\in U
the pair
(x,0)
is an interior point of
the given thickening
S
, we can assume to have chosen
a_{x}
and

d_{x}
so that
T\subseteq S\subseteq\langle\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.
In case
U
is large open, we can proceed as above to obtain
a_{x}
,

d_{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, so that
T
would then be a large thickening.

Let us consider the net of smooth function
r_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\text{\rm th}_{v_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))

defined by
r_{\varepsilon}(y,h):=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial f_{\varepsilon}}{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}(y+thv_{\varepsilon})\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}

for all
\varepsilon\in I
. We calculate the partial derivative
\partial^{\alpha}r_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n+1}
and an arbitrary point
(y,h)\in T
. For
simplicity, set
\hat{\alpha}:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
, and

v_{\varepsilon}=:(v_{1\varepsilon},\ldots,v_{n\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

\partial^{\alpha}r_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial h^{\alpha_{n+1}}\partial y^{\hat{\alpha}}}\left[\frac{\partial f{}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}(y_{\varepsilon}+th_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})\right]\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}

(4.1)

Applying the chain rule and the mean value theorem for integrals,
(
4.1
) can be written as a sum of terms of
the form
\partial^{\beta}f(y_{\varepsilon}+t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})v_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma}t_{\varepsilon}^{m}
,
for suitable multi-indices
\beta,\gamma
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Here,
t_{\varepsilon}\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}

for all
\varepsilon\in I
. From
(y,h)\in T
, we get
y\in B_{a_{x}}(x)

and
h\in B_{d_{x}}(0)
for some
x\in U
. This gives
|y+thv-x|\leq|y-x|+|hv|<a_{x}+b_{x}<R_{x}
,
so that
y+thv\in B_{R_{x}}(x)\subseteq U
. From Def.
14

(ii)
we hence have that
\left(\partial^{\beta}f_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon}+t_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})\right)

is
\rho
-moderate. Since moderateness is preserved by polynomials,
and
t_{\varepsilon}\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
is moderate, from (
4.1
)
we obtain that
\left(\partial^{\alpha}r_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon},h_{\varepsilon})\right)

is moderate. This proves that
r:=[r_{\varepsilon}(-,-)]|_{T}:T\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

is a GSF.

We have

\displaystyle h\cdot r(x,h)

\displaystyle=\left[h_{\varepsilon}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial f_{\varepsilon}}{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon}+th_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\int_{0}^{h_{\varepsilon}}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}+sv_{\varepsilon})\right\}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right]

\displaystyle=[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}+h_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})]-[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]=f(x+hv)-f(x).

Of course
r(x,0)=\left[\frac{\partial f_{\varepsilon}}{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon})\right]
,
and this concludes the existence part.

To prove uniqueness, consider
(x,h)\in T\cap\bar{T}
, where
T

and
\bar{T}
are two thickenings (along
v
) of the incremental
ratios
r
,
\bar{r}
. Define
R(k):=r(x,k)
and
\bar{R}(k):=\bar{r}(x,k)

for
k\in B_{b}(0)
, where
(x,h)\in B_{a}(x)\times B_{b}(0)\subseteq T\cap\bar{T}

by the definition of thickening. Since
k\in B_{b}(0)\mapsto(x,k)\in T\cap\bar{T}

is a GSF, both
R
and
\bar{R}
are still generalized smooth maps
by the closure with respect to composition. Moreover

k\cdot R(k)=k\cdot r(x,k)=f(x+kv)-f(x),

(4.2)

and analogously
k\cdot\bar{R}(k)=f(x+kv)-f(x)=k\cdot R(k)
. Therefore

R(k)=\bar{R}(k)
for every
k\in B_{b}(0)
which is invertible,
and Lemma
34
yields
R=\bar{R}
. Since

h\in B_{b}(0)
we get
R(h)=r(x,h)=\bar{R}(h)=\bar{r}(x,h)
.
∎

We will use the notation
\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}[-,-]_{T}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(T,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

(or simply
\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}[-,-]
in case the domain
is clear from the context) for the generalized smooth incremental
ratio of a function
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
defined on the
thickening
T
, so as to distinguish it from the derivative
\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.
Since any partial derivative of a GSF is still a GSF, higher order
derivatives
\frac{\partial^{\alpha}f}{\partial v^{\alpha}}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

are simply defined recursively.

As follows from Thm.
33

(i)

and Thm.
25

(v)
, the concept
of derivative defined using the Fermat-Reyes theorem is compatible
with the classical derivative of Schwartz distributions via the embeddings

\iota^{b}
from Thm.
25
. The following result
follows from the analogous properties for the nets of smooth functions
defining
f
and
g
.

Theorem 36
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an
open subset in the sharp topology, let
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
f
,

g:U\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
be generalized smooth maps. Then

(i)

\frac{\partial(f+g)}{\partial v}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}

(ii)

\frac{\partial(r\cdot f)}{\partial v}=r\cdot\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\quad\forall r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

(iii)

\frac{\partial(f\cdot g)}{\partial v}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\cdot g+f\cdot\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}

(iv)

For each
x\in U
, the map
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}(x).v:=\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}(x)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

is
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
-linear in
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Using the Fermat-Reyes theorem, it is also possible to give intrinsic
proofs (i.e. without using nets of smooth functions that define a
given GSF), as exemplified in the following

Theorem 37
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

be open subsets in the sharp topology and
g\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(V,U)
,

f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be generalized smooth maps. Then
for all
x\in V
and all
v\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\left(f\circ g\right)}{\partial v}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}\left(g(x)\right).\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}(x)

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\!\left(f\circ g\right)}{\hbox{\rm d}\!\left(f\circ g\right)}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\!\left(f\circ g\right)$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\!\left(f\circ g\right)$}}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}\left(g(x)\right)\circ\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}g}{\hbox{\rm d}g}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}g$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}g$}}(x).

Proof.

For
h
small (in the sharp topology), we can write

f\left[g(x+hv)\right]=f\left[g(x)+h\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}[x,h]\right].

(4.3)

Set
u(x,h):=\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}[x,h]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then
(
4.3
) yields

f\left[g(x+hv)\right]=f(g(x))+h\cdot\frac{\partial f}{\partial u(x,h)}\left[g(x),h\right].

Therefore, the uniqueness of the smooth incremental ratio of
f\circ g

in the direction
v
implies

\frac{\partial\left(f\circ g\right)}{\partial v}[x,h]=\frac{\partial f}{\partial u(x,h)}\left[g(x),h\right].

For
h=0
, we get

\frac{\partial\left(f\circ g\right)}{\partial v}(x)=\frac{\partial f}{\partial u(x,0)}\left(g(x)\right)=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}(g(x)).u(x,0)=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}(g(x)).\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}(x),

which is our conclusion.
∎

5.
Integral calculus using primitives

In this section, we inquire existence and uniqueness of primitives

F
of a GSF
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
(see also
[
120
]
for
an analogous approach). To this end, we shall have to introduce the
derivative
F^{\prime}(x)
at boundary points
x\in[a,b]
, i.e. such that

x-a
or
b-x
is not invertible. Let us note explicitly, in fact,
that the Fermat-Reyes Theorem
33
is stated only
for sharply open domains. We shal therefore require the following
result.

Lemma 38
.

Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

be such that
a<b
, then the interior
\text{int}\left([a,b]\right)

in the sharp topology is dense in
[a,b]
.

Proof.

Take representatives of
a
,
b
and
x\in[a,b]
such that
a_{\varepsilon}<b_{\varepsilon}

and
a_{\varepsilon}\leq x_{\varepsilon}\leq b_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon
small. Thm.
10

(ii)
yields
\text{int}\left([a,b]\right)=\langle(a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon})\rangle
.
To prove the conclusion, it suffices to define

y_{k\varepsilon}:=\begin{cases}x_{\varepsilon}&\text{if }a_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}\leq x_{\varepsilon}\leq b_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}\\
a_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}&\text{if }x_{\varepsilon}<a_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}\\
b_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}&\text{if }x_{\varepsilon}>b_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}\end{cases}

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in I
. We have
d(y_{k\varepsilon},(a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon})^{c})\geq\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}
,
so that
y_{k}\in\langle(a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon})\rangle
. Moreover,
|y_{k\varepsilon}-x_{\varepsilon}|<\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}

for all
\varepsilon
, and from this the desired limit condition follows.
∎

The following result shows that every GSF can have at most one primitive
GSF up to an additive constant.

Theorem 39
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
and
let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be a generalized smooth function.
Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, with
a<b
, such that
(a,b)\subseteq X
.
If
f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\in\text{\emph{int}}(a,b)
, then
f
is
constant on
(a,b)
. An analogous statement holds if we take any
other type of interval (closed or half closed) instead of
(a,b)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
18
, we can assume that
f
is
defined by a net of smooth functions
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
.
From the Fermat-Reyes Theorem
33
, we know that
f^{\prime}(x)=[f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]

for every interior point
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in X
. For all
x
,
y\in\text{int}(a,b)\subseteq U
,
we can write

\displaystyle f(x)-f(y)

\displaystyle=[f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})-f_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})]=\left[(y_{\varepsilon}-x_{\varepsilon})\cdot\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}+s(y_{\varepsilon}-x_{\varepsilon}))\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right]

\displaystyle=(y-x)\cdot[f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}+s_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon}-x_{\varepsilon}))]=(y-x)\cdot f^{\prime}(x+s(y-x)),

(5.1)

where
s_{\varepsilon}\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
is provided by the integral mean value
theorem and
s:=[s_{\varepsilon}]\in[0,1]
. Since
x
,
y\in\text{int}(a,b)
,
we have
x+s(y-x)\in\text{int}(a,b)
and hence
f^{\prime}(x+s(y-x))=0
.
Thereby, (
5.1
) yields
f(x)=f(y)
as claimed. For a different
type of interval, it suffices to consider Lemma
38

and sharp continuity of GSF (Thm.
17
).
∎

Remark 40
.

From the Fermat-Reyes Thm.
33

and from Thm.
39
, it follows that
the function
i(x):=1
if
x\approx 0
and
i(x):=0
otherwise cannot
be a GSF on any large neighborhood of
x=0
. This example stems from
the property that different standard real numbers can always be separated
by infinitesimal balls.

At interior points
x\in[a,b]
in the sharp topology, the definition
of derivative
f^{(k)}(x)
follows from the Fermat-Reyes Theorem

33
. At boundary points, we have the following

Theorem 41
.

Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

with
a<b
, and
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be a generalized
smooth function. Then for all
x\in[a,b]
, the following limit exists
in the sharp topology

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\to x\\
y\in\text{\emph{int}}\left([a,b]\right)\end{subarray}}f^{(k)}(y)=:f^{(k)}(x).

Moreover, if the net
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R})
defines

f
and
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]
, then
f^{(k)}(x)=[f_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(x_{\varepsilon})]

and hence
f^{(k)}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.

Proof.

We have

{\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\to x\\
y\in\text{int}\left([a,b]\right)\end{subarray}}}f^{(k)}(y)={\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\to x\\
y\in\text{int}\left([a,b]\right)\end{subarray}}}\left[f_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(y_{\varepsilon})\right]=[f{}_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(x_{\varepsilon})],

where the last equality follows due to the sharp continuity of
[f_{\varepsilon}^{(k)}(-)]

at every point
x\in[a,b]\subseteq\langle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\rangle
(Thm.
17

(iii)
and Lem.
38
).
∎

We can now prove existence and uniqueness of primitives of GSF:

Theorem 42
.

Let
a
,
b
,
c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
with
a<b
and
c\in[a,b]
. Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

be a generalized smooth function. Then, there exists one and only
one generalized smooth function
F\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

such that
F(c)=0
and
F^{\prime}(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in[a,b]
. Moreover,
if
f
is defined by the net
f_{\varepsilon}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{C}$}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
and
c=[c_{\varepsilon}]
,
then
F(x)=\left[\int_{c_{\varepsilon}}^{x_{\varepsilon}}f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right]

for all
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in[a,b]
.

Proof.

Fix representatives
(a_{\varepsilon})
,
(b_{\varepsilon})
and
(c_{\varepsilon})

of
a
,
b
,
c
such that

a_{\varepsilon}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\leq b_{\varepsilon}

(5.2)

for
\varepsilon
small. By Lemma
18
, we can assume
that
f
is generated by a net
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
. Set

F_{\varepsilon}(x):=\int_{c_{\varepsilon}}^{x}f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.3)

We want to prove that the net
(F_{\varepsilon})
defines a GSF of type

[a,b]\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and therefore we take
x\in[a,b]

and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
. Choose a representative
(x_{\varepsilon})
of
x

such that

a_{\varepsilon}\leq x_{\varepsilon}\leq b_{\varepsilon}

(5.4)

for
\varepsilon
small. If
\alpha>0
, then
F_{\varepsilon}^{(\alpha)}(x_{\varepsilon})=f_{\varepsilon}^{(\alpha-1)}(x_{\varepsilon})

and hence moderateness is clear since
x\in[a,b]
. For
\alpha=0

we have
F_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})=f_{\varepsilon}(\sigma_{\varepsilon})\cdot(x_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon})
,
where

\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in[c_{\varepsilon},x_{\varepsilon}]\cup[x_{\varepsilon},c_{\varepsilon}]\quad\forall\varepsilon\in I

(5.5)

is obtained by the integral mean value theorem. For
\varepsilon
small,
we have both (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), so that these
inequalities and (
5.5
) yield
\sigma\in[a,b]\subseteq U
.
Therefore
(f_{\varepsilon}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}))
and
(F_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}))

are moderate. This proves condition Def.
14

(ii)

for the net
(F_{\varepsilon})
, and we can hence set
F(x):=[F_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

for all
x=[x_{\varepsilon}]\in[a,b]
.

If
y\in\text{int}([a,b])
, we can apply our differential calculus
to the generalized smooth map
F|_{\text{int}([a,b])}=[F_{\varepsilon}(-)]|_{\text{int}([a,b])}
,
obtaining
F^{\prime}(y)=[f_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})]=f(y)
. From this, if
x\in[a,b]
,
we get

F^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\to x\\
y\in\text{int}([a,b])\end{subarray}}F^{\prime}(y)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\to x\\
y\in\text{int}([a,b])\end{subarray}}f(y)=f(x)

because
f
is sharply continuous at
x\in[a,b]\subseteq U
. The
uniqueness part follows from Theorem
39
.
∎

Definition 43
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
42
,
we denote by
\int_{c}^{(-)}f:=\int_{c}^{(-)}f(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

the unique generalized smooth function such that:

(i)

\int_{c}^{c}f=0

(ii)

\left(\int_{c}^{(-)}f\right)^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}\int_{c}^{x}f(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}=f(x)

for all
x\in[a,b]
.

In Sec.
7
, we develop a generalization of
this concept of integration to GSF in several variables and to more
general domains of integration
M\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Example 44
.

(i)

Since
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
contains both infinitesimal and infinite numbers,
our notion of definite integral also includes “improper integrals”.
Let e.g.
f(x)=\frac{1}{x}
for
x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and
a=1
,
b=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-q}
,

q>0
. Then

\int_{a}^{b}f(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}=\left[\int_{1}^{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-q}}\frac{1}{s}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\right]=[\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-q}]-\log 1=-q\log\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}},

(5.6)

which is, of course, a positive infinite generalized number. This
apparently trivial result is closely tied to the possibility to define
GSF on arbitrary domains, like
F\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}([a,b],\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

in Thm.
42
where
b
is an
infinite number as in (
5.6
), which is one of the key
properties allowing one to get the closure with respect to composition.

(ii)

If
p
,
q\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
p<0<q
and both
p
and
q
are not infinitesimal,
then
\int_{p}^{q}\delta(t)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\approx 1
.

Theorem 45
.

Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
and
g\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(Y,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

be generalized smooth functions defined on arbitrary domains in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
with
a<b
and
[a,b]\subseteq X\cap Y
,
then

(i)

\int_{a}^{b}\left(f+g\right)=\int_{a}^{b}f+\int_{a}^{b}g

(ii)

\int_{a}^{b}\lambda f=\lambda\int_{a}^{b}f\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

(iii)

\int_{a}^{b}f=\int_{a}^{c}f+\int_{c}^{b}f

for all
c\in[a,b]

(iv)

\int_{a}^{b}f=-\int_{b}^{a}f

(v)

\int_{a}^{b}f^{\prime}=f(b)-f(a)

(vi)

\int_{a}^{b}f^{\prime}\cdot g=\left[f\cdot g\right]_{a}^{b}-\int_{a}^{b}f\cdot g^{\prime}

(vii)

If
f(x)\leq g(x)
for all
x\in[a,b]
, then

\int_{a}^{b}f\leq\int_{a}^{b}g
.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
5.3
) and the usual rules
of the integral calculus, or from Def.
43
and Thm.
33

for property
(vii)
.
∎

Theorem 46
.

Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(T,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

and
\varphi\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(S,T)
be generalized smooth functions
defined on arbitrary domains in
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
with
a<b
, such that
[a,b]\subseteq S
,
\varphi(a)<\varphi(b)
and

[\varphi(a),\varphi(b)]\subseteq T
. Finally, assume that
\varphi([a,b])\subseteq[\varphi(a),\varphi(b)]
.
Then

\int_{\varphi(a)}^{\varphi(b)}f(t)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}=\int_{a}^{b}f\left[\varphi(s)\right]\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}.

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle F(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\varphi(a)}^{x}f(t)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\quad\forall x\in[\varphi(a),\varphi(b)]

\displaystyle H(y)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\varphi(a)}^{\varphi(y)}f(t)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\quad\forall y\in[a,b]

\displaystyle G(y)

\displaystyle:=\int_{a}^{y}f\left[\varphi(s)\right]\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\quad\forall y\in[a,b],

Each one of these functions is generalized smooth by Def.
43

of the integral or by Thm.
28
, because it can
be written as a composition of generalized smooth maps. We have
H(a)=G(a)=0
,

H(y)=F\left[\varphi(y)\right]
for every
y\in[a,b]
and, by the chain
rule (Prop.
37
)
H^{\prime}(y)=F^{\prime}[\varphi(y)]\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(y)=f\left[\varphi(y)\right]\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(y)=G^{\prime}(y)
,
the last two equalities following by Def.
43
of
the integral. From the uniqueness Theorem
39
,
the conclusion
H=G
follows.

∎

Remark 47
.

(Relation to distributional primitives) Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a<b
, and
set
\Omega=(a,b)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. By
[
104
, Ch. II, §4]
there exists
a sequentially continuous operator
R:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
assigning
to any
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
a primitive
R(T)
, i.e.,
R(T)^{\prime}=T
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
.
Now let
\iota_{\Omega}^{b}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\to{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\text{\rm c}}({\Omega}),\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

be an embedding as in Theorem
25
, and fix any

c\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
with
a\leq c\leq b
. Then

\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(R(T))^{\prime}=\iota_{\Omega}^{b}(R(T)^{\prime})=\iota(T)=\Big(\int_{c}^{(-)}\iota(T)\Big)^{\prime}.

Therefore, Theorem
45

(v)
implies
that

\int_{r}^{s}\iota(T)=\iota(R(T))(s)-\iota(R(T))(r)

for all
s,t\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
with
a\leq s,t\leq b
.

6.
Some classical theorems for generalized
smooth functions

It is natural to expect that several classical theorems of differential
and integral calculus can be extended from the ordinary smooth case
to the generalized smooth framework. Once again, we underscore that
these faithful generalizations are possible because we don’t have
a priori limitations in the evaluation
f(x)
for GSF. For example,
one does not have similar results in Colombeau theory, where an arbitrary
generalized function can be evaluated only at compactly supported
points.

We start from the intermediate value theorem.

Corollary 48
.

Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be a
generalized smooth function defined on the subset
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, with
a<b
, such that
[a,b]\subseteq X
.
Assume that
f(a)<f(b)
. Then

\forall y\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}:\ f(a)\leq y\leq f(b)\ \Rightarrow\ \exists c\in[a,b]:\ y=f(c).

Proof.

Let
f
be defined by the net
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
. For small

\varepsilon
and for suitable representatives
(a_{\varepsilon})
,
(b_{\varepsilon})
,

(y_{\varepsilon})
, we have

a_{\varepsilon}<b_{\varepsilon}\quad,\quad f_{\varepsilon}(a_{\varepsilon})\leq y_{\varepsilon}\leq f_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}).

By the classical intermediate value theorem we get some
c_{\varepsilon}\in[a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon}]

such that
f_{\varepsilon}(c_{\varepsilon})=y_{\varepsilon}
. Therefore
c:=[c_{\varepsilon}]\in[a,b]\subseteq X

and hence
f(c)=[f_{\varepsilon}(c_{\varepsilon})]=[y_{\varepsilon}]=y
.
∎

Using this theorem we can conclude that no GSF can assume
only a finite number of values which are comparable with respect to
the relation
<
on any nontrivial interval
[a,b]\subseteq X
,
unless it is constant. For example, this provides an alternative way
of seeing that the function
i
of Rem.
40
cannot
be a generalized smooth map.

We note that the solution
c\in[a,b]
of the previous generalized
smooth equation
y=f(x)
need not even be continuous in
\varepsilon
.
Indeed, let us consider the net of smooth functions depicted in Figure

6.1
, where it is understood that, as
\varepsilon
approaches

0
, the two waves at the extremes oscillate around the dashed rectilinear
positions shown in the figure. Set
f(x)=\left[\int_{0}^{1}f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}-f_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})\right]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

for
x\in[0,1]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and analyze the generalized smooth
equation
f(x)=0
. Let
\varepsilon_{k}=\frac{1}{k}
be the “times”
where the two waves of the net
(f_{\varepsilon})
are rectilinear. At these
times the solution
f(x_{\varepsilon_{k}})=0
can be any point
x_{\varepsilon_{k}}\in[b,c]
.
Assume that for
\varepsilon\in\left[\frac{1}{k},\frac{1}{k}+\delta_{k}\right]

only the wave on the left is rectilinear and for
\varepsilon\in\left[\frac{1}{k}-\delta_{k},\frac{1}{k}\right]

only the wave on the right is rectilinear (where
\delta_{k}\downarrow 0

is sufficiently small). Therefore, in the first case, any solution
must be of the form
x_{\varepsilon}\in[c,1]
and in the second case
x_{\varepsilon}\in[0,b]
.
Thus any solution must jump at every time
\varepsilon_{k}
and the height
of the jump must be at least
c-b
.

This example allows us to draw the following general conclusion: if
we consider generalized numbers as solutions of smooth equations,
then we are forced to work on a non-totally ordered ring of scalars
derived from discontinuous (in
\varepsilon
) representatives. To put it
differently: if we choose a ring of scalars with a total order or
continuous representatives, we will not be able to solve every smooth
equation, and the given ring can be considered, in some sense, incomplete.
Of course, this does not mean that the study of better behaved (non-totally
ordered) subrings of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, useful for special purposes, is not
interesting.

Figure 6.1.
A net
(f_{\varepsilon})
defining a discontinuous solution
of a smooth equation.

Theorem 49
.

Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d})

be a generalized smooth function defined in the sharply open set
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
[a,b]\subseteq X
. Then

(i)

If
n=d=1
, then
\exists c\in[a,b]:\ f(b)-f(a)=(b-a)\cdot f^{\prime}(c)
.

(ii)

If
n=d=1
, then
\exists c\in[a,b]:\ \int_{a}^{b}f(t)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}=(b-a)\cdot f(c)
.

(iii)

If
d=1
, then
\exists c\in[a,b]:\ f(b)-f(a)=\nabla f(c)\cdot(b-a)
.

(iv)

Let
h:=b-a
, then
f(a+h)-f(a)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\hbox{\rm d}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}f$}}(a+t\cdot h).h\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}
.

Proof.

Using the usual notations, for small
\varepsilon
we have
a_{\varepsilon}<b_{\varepsilon}

and

\displaystyle\exists c_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\in[a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon}]:\ f_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon})-f_{\varepsilon}(a_{\varepsilon})=(b_{\varepsilon}-a_{\varepsilon})\cdot f^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(c_{\varepsilon})

(6.1)

\displaystyle\exists c_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\in[a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon}]:\ \int_{a_{\varepsilon}}^{b_{\varepsilon}}f_{\varepsilon}=(b_{\varepsilon}-a_{\varepsilon})\cdot f{}_{\varepsilon}(c_{\varepsilon}),

(6.2)

from which the conclusions
(i)
and
(ii)

follow directly. The several variables and vector valued cases
(iii)
,

(iv)
follow as usual by reduction to the one-variable
and scalar valued case.
∎

Internal sets generated by a sharply bounded net of compact sets serve
as a substitute for compact subsets for GSF, as can be seen from the
following extreme value theorem:

Lemma 50
.

Let
\emptyset\neq K=[K_{\varepsilon}]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

be an internal set generated by a sharply bounded net
(K_{\varepsilon})

of compact sets
K_{\varepsilon}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
Assume that
\alpha:K\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

is a well-defined map given by
\alpha(x)=[\alpha_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]

for all
x\in K
, where
\alpha_{\varepsilon}:K_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
are continuous
maps (e.g.
\alpha(x)=|x|
). Then

\exists m,M\in K\,\forall x\in K:\ \alpha(m)\leq\alpha(x)\leq\alpha(M).

Proof.

Since
K\neq\emptyset
, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, let us say
for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,
K_{\varepsilon}
is non empty and, by our assumptions,
it is also compact. Since each
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
is continuous, for
all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
we have

\exists m_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}\in K_{\varepsilon}\,\forall x\in K_{\varepsilon}:\ \alpha_{\varepsilon}(m_{\varepsilon})\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\alpha_{\varepsilon}(M_{\varepsilon}).

Since the net
(K_{\varepsilon})
is sharply bounded, both the nets
(m_{\varepsilon})

and
(M_{\varepsilon})
are moderate. Therefore
m=[m_{\varepsilon}]
,
M=[M_{\varepsilon}]\in K
.
Take any
x\in[K_{\varepsilon}]
, then there exists a representative
(x_{\varepsilon})

such that
x_{\varepsilon}\in K_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon
small. Therefore
\alpha(m)=[\alpha_{\varepsilon}(m_{\varepsilon})]\leq[\alpha_{\varepsilon}(x{}_{\varepsilon})]=\alpha(x)\leq\alpha(M)
.
∎

Corollary 51
.

Let
f\in{}^{\rho}\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be
a generalized smooth function defined in the subset
X\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let
\emptyset\neq K=[K_{\varepsilon}]\subseteq X
be an internal set generated
by a sharply bounded net
(K_{\varepsilon})
of compact sets
K_{\varepsilon}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
then

\exists m,M\in K\,\forall x\in K:\ f(m)\leq f(x)\leq f(M).

(6.3)

These results motivate the following

Definition 52
.

A subset
K
of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is called
functionally
compact
, denoted by
K\Subset_{\text{f}}\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, if there exists a net
(K_{\varepsilon})

such that

(i)

K=[K_{\varepsilon}]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(ii)

(K_{\varepsilon})
is sharply bounded

(iii)

\forall\varepsilon\in I:\ K_{\varepsilon}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

If, in addition,
K\subseteq U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
then we write

K\Subset_{\text{f}}U
. Finally, we write
[K_{\varepsilon}]\Subset_{\text{f}}U
if
(ii)
,

(iii)
and
[K_{\varepsilon}]\subseteq U
hold.

We refer to
[
40
]
for a deeper study of this type of compact
sets in the case
\rho=(\varepsilon)
. Note that any interval
[a,b]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

with
b-a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, is
not
connected: in fact if
c\in(a,b)
,
then both
c+D_{\infty}
and
[a,b]\setminus\left(c+D_{\infty}\right)

are sharply open in
[a,b]
. Once again, this is a general property
in several non-Archimedean frameworks (see e.g.
[
92
,
61
]
).
On the other hand, as in the case of functionally compact sets, GSF
behave on intervals as if they were connected, in the sense that both
the intermediate value theorem Cor.
48
and the
extreme value theorem Cor.
51
hold for them
(therefore,
f\left([a,b]\right)=\left[f(m),f(M)\right]
, where we
used the notations from the results just mentioned).

We close this section with generalizations of Taylor’s theorem in
various forms. In the following statement,
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}(x):\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{dk}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

is the
k
-th differential of the GSF
f
, viewed as an
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
-multilinear
map
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\displaystyle\mathop{\ldots\ldots\,}^{k}\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and we use the
common notation
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}(x)\cdot h^{k}:=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}(x)(h,\ldots,h)
.
Clearly,
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{k}f$}}(x)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{dk},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
. For multilinear
maps
A:\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
, we set
|A|:=[|A_{\varepsilon}|]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
the generalized number defined by the norms of the operators
A_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Theorem 53
.

Let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be a generalized smooth
function defined in the sharply open set
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Let
a
,
b\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that the line segment
[a,b]\subseteq U
,
and set
h:=b-a
. Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(i)

\exists\xi\in[a,b]:\ f(a+h)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}(a)}{j!}\cdot h^{j}+\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}\cdot h^{n+1}.

(ii)

f(a+h)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}(a)}{j!}\cdot h^{j}+\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{n}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(a+th)\cdot h^{n+1}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}.

Moreover, there exists some
R\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that

\forall k\in B_{R}(0)\,\exists\xi\in[a,a+k]:\ f(a+k)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}(a)}{j!}\cdot k^{j}+\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}\cdot k^{n+1}

(6.4)

\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}\cdot k^{n+1}=\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{n}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(a+tk)\cdot k^{n+1}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\approx 0.

(6.5)

Formulas
(i)
and
(ii)
correspond
to a plain generalization of Taylor’s theorem for ordinary smooth
functions with Lagrange and integral remainder, respectively. Dealing
with generalized functions, it is important to note that this direct
statement also includes the possibility that the differential
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)

may be infinite at some point. For this reason, in (
6.4
)
and (
6.5
), considering a sufficiently small
increment
k
, we get more classical infinitesimal remainders
\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)\cdot k^{n+1}\approx 0
.

Proof.

Let
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
be a net of smooth functions
that defines
f
. We have
a+h=b\in[a,b]\subseteq U
and
U
is
sharply open, so by the Taylor formula applied to
f_{\varepsilon}
and
by Theorem
33
we have

\displaystyle f(a+h)

\displaystyle=[f_{\varepsilon}(a_{\varepsilon}+h_{\varepsilon})]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f_{\varepsilon}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f_{\varepsilon}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f_{\varepsilon}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f_{\varepsilon}$}}(a_{\varepsilon})}{j!}h_{\varepsilon}^{j}+\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}$}}(\xi_{\varepsilon})}{(n+1)!}h_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{j}f$}}(a)}{j!}h^{j}+\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}h^{n+1}

for some
\xi_{\varepsilon}\in(a_{\varepsilon},b_{\varepsilon})
, and where
\xi=[\xi_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

so that
\xi\in[a,b]
. Analogously, we can prove
(ii)
.

To prove the second part of the theorem, we start by considering a
sharp ball
B_{r}(a)\subseteq U
, where
r=[r_{\varepsilon}]>0
. Set
H:=\left[\overline{B^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm E}}}_{r_{\varepsilon}/2}(a_{\varepsilon})}\right]
,
and

K:=\max\left(\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(M)\right|,\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(m)\right|\right)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},

where
\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(M)\right|
and
\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(m)\right|

are the maximum and the minimum values of
\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}\right|=\left[\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f_{\varepsilon}$}}\right|\right]:U\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

on
H\subseteq U
, see Cor.
51
. We hence have

\left|\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)\right|\leq K
for all
\xi\in H
. Take
any strictly positive number
P\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
P\geq K

and any strictly positive infinitesimal
p\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
so that

\frac{p}{P}\approx 0
and hence
\left(\frac{p}{P}\right)^{n+1}\leq\frac{p}{P}
.
Set
R:=\min\left(\frac{r}{2},\frac{p}{P}\right)
, then
R\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

since both
r
and
\frac{p}{P}
are invertible. If
k\in B_{R}(0)

then
[a,a+k]\subseteq H\subseteq U
. We can therefore apply
(i)

to get (
6.4
). Finally

\left|\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}^{n+1}f$}}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}k^{n+1}\right|\leq\frac{K}{(n+1)!}R^{n+1}\leq\frac{P}{(n+1)!}\cdot\left(\frac{p}{P}\right)^{n+1}\leq\frac{P}{(n+1)!}\cdot\frac{p}{P}\approx 0.

∎

The following definitions allow us to state Taylor formulas in Peano
and in infinitesimal form. The latter has no remainder term thanks
to the use of an equivalence relation that permits the introduction
of a language of nilpotent infinitesimals, see e.g.
[
34
]

for a similar formulation. For simplicity, we only present the 1-dimentional
case.

Definition 54
.

(i)

Let
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
be a sharp neighborhood
of
0
and
P
,
Q:U\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
be maps defined on
U
.
Then we say that

P(u)=o(Q(u))\quad\text{as }u\to 0

if there exists a function
R:U\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\forall u\in U:\ P(u)=R(u)\cdot Q(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{u\to 0}R(u)=0,

where the limit is taken in the sharp topology.

(ii)

Let
x
,
y\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
and

k
,
j\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then we write
x=_{j}y
if there exist representatives

(x_{\varepsilon})
,
(y_{\varepsilon})
of
x
,
y
, respectively, such that

|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|=O(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}).

(6.6)

We will read
x=_{j}y
as
x

is equal to

y

up to

j+1
-th order infinitesimals
. Finally, if
k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
we set
D_{kj}:=\left\{x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\mid x^{k+1}=_{j}0\right\}
, which
is called the
set of

k
-th order infinitesimals for
the equality
=_{j}
, and

D_{\infty j}:=\left\{x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\mid\exists k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}:\ x^{k+1}=_{j}0\right\}

which is called the
set of infinitesimals for the equality
=_{j}
.

Of course, the reformulation of Def.
54

(i)
for the classical Landau’s little-oh is particularly
suited to the case of a ring like
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, instead of a field. The
intuitive interpretation of
x=_{j}y
is that for particular (physics-related)
problems one is not interested in distinguishing quantities whose
difference
|x-y|
is less than an infinitesimal of order
j
. The
idea behind taking
\frac{1}{j}
in (
6.6
) is
to obtain the property that the greater the order
j
of the infinitesimal
error, the greater the difference
|x-y|
is allowed to be. This
is a typical property in rings with nilpotent infinitesimals (see
e.g.
[
34
,
61
]
). The set
D_{ki}
represents the neighborhood
of infinitesimals of
k
-th order for the equality
=_{j}
. Once
again, the greater the order
k
, the bigger is the neighborhood
(see Theorem
55

(viii)

below).

Theorem 55
.

Let
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(U,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
be
a generalized smooth function defined in the sharply open set
U\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
x
,
\delta\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, with
\delta>0
and
[x-\delta,x+\delta]\subseteq U
.
Let
k
,
l
,
j\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then

(i)

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\ f(x+u)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}+o(u^{n})

as
u\to 0
.

(ii)

The definition of
x=_{j}y
does not depend
on the representatives of
x
,
y
.

(iii)

=_{j}
is an equivalence relation on

\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.

(iv)

If
x=_{j}y
and
l\geq j
,
then
x=_{l}y
.

(v)

If
\forall^{0}j\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\ x=_{j}y
,
then
x=y
.

(vi)

If
x=_{j}y
and
z=_{j}w
then

x+z=_{j}y+w
. If
x
and
z
are finite, then
x\cdot z=_{j}y\cdot w
.

(vii)

\forall h\in D_{kj}:\ h\approx 0
.

(viii)

D_{mj}\subseteq D_{kj}\subseteq D_{\infty j}

if
m\leq k
.

(ix)

D_{kj}
is a subring of
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
. For
all
h\in D_{kj}
and all finite
x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
x\cdot h\in D_{kj}
.

(x)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and assume that
j
satisfies

\forall z\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\,\forall\xi\in[x-\delta,x+\delta]:\ z=_{j}0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad z\cdot f^{(n+1)}(\xi)=_{j}0.

(6.7)

Then there exist
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
k\leq n
and

\forall u\in D_{kj}:\ f(x+u)=_{j}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}.

(xi)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
there exist
e
,
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

such that
e\leq j
,
k\leq n
and
\forall u\in D_{ke}:\ f(x+u)=_{e}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}
.

Proof.

In order to prove
(i)
we set
P(u)=f(x+u)-\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}
,

Q(u)=u^{n}
and
R(u)=u\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\frac{f^{(n+1)}(x+tu)}{n!}(1-t)^{n}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}

for
u\in B_{\delta}(0)
. The segment
[x-u,x+u]\subseteq B_{\delta}(x)\subseteq U
,
so Thm.
53

(ii)
yields
P(u)=Q(u)\cdot R(u)

for all
u\in U_{x}
. As in the previous proof, set

K:=\max\left(\left|f^{(n+1)}(M)\right|,\left|f^{(n+1)}(m)\right|\right)

so that
\left|f^{(n+1)}(\xi)\right|\leq K
for all
\xi\in[x-\delta,x+\delta]
,
then

|R(u)|\leq|u|\cdot\left|\int_{0}^{1}\frac{f^{(n+1)}(x+tu)}{n!}(1-t)^{n}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}t$}}\right|\leq|u|\cdot\frac{K}{(n+1)!}

which goes to
0
as
u\to 0
in the sharp topology.

The proofs of
(ii)
-
(ix)
are
simple. We only prove that
D_{kj}
is closed with respect to sums.
Let
x
,
y\in D_{kj}
so that

\left|\frac{x_{\varepsilon}^{k+1}}{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}}\right|\leq M\quad,\quad\left|\frac{y_{\varepsilon}^{k+1}}{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}}\right|\leq N

(6.8)

for
\varepsilon
small and for some
M
,
N\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\frac{(x_{\varepsilon}+y_{\varepsilon})^{k+1}}{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{k+1}{k+1\choose r}\left|\frac{x_{\varepsilon}^{k+1}}{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}}\right|^{\frac{r}{k+1}}\left|\frac{y_{\varepsilon}^{k+1}}{\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{j}}}\right|^{\frac{k+1-r}{k+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{k+1}{k+1\choose r}M^{\frac{r}{k+1}}N^{\frac{k+1-r}{k+1}},

proving the claim.

In order to show
(x)
, we first note that
x=_{j}y

is equivalent to

\exists A\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\ |x-y|\leq A\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{}^{\frac{1}{j}}.

We again use the notation
K:=\max\left(\left|f^{(n+1)}(M)\right|,\left|f^{(n+1)}(m)\right|\right)

and set
p:=\inf\left\{q\in\mathbb{R}\mid\exists B\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\ K\leq B\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{}^{-q}\right\}

so that for any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have

\displaystyle\left|f(x+u)-\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{(n+1)!}\cdot|u|^{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq B\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-p-r}\cdot|u|^{n+1}

(6.9)

for some
B\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Now assume that
u\in D_{kj}
. Then

|u|^{k+1}\leq A\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{\frac{1}{j}}

for some
A\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, so that

|u|^{n+1}\leq A\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{\frac{n+1}{j(k+1)}}.

This and (
6.9
) yield

\left|f(x+u)-\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(r)}(x)}{r!}u^{r}\right|\leq AB\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{\frac{n+1}{j(k+1)}-p-r},

which gives the desired conclusion if
\frac{n+1}{j(k+1)}-p-r\geq\frac{1}{j}
.
There exists such an
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
if and only if
\frac{n+1}{j(k+1)}-p-\frac{1}{j}>0
,
i.e. for
(k+1)(jp+1)<n+1
. If
jp+1\leq 0
, then any
k>0
satisfies
this inequality. Otherwise, we need to prove the existence of
k<\frac{n-jp}{jp+1}
.
Since
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, a sufficient condition for this is that
jp<1
.
This gives a relation between the order of the equality
=_{j}
and
the order of infinity
p
of the derivative
f^{(n+1)}
on the interval

[x-\delta,x+\delta]
. In order to prove that
jp<1
, we thus need
to use the assumption (
6.7
).
Set
z:=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{\frac{s}{j}}
, where
1\leq s<2
. Then
0\leq z\leq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{\frac{1}{j}}
,
i.e.
z=_{j}0
, and (
6.7
)
yields

\forall\xi\in[x-\delta,x+\delta]:\ z\cdot\left|f^{(n+1)}(\xi)\right|=_{j}0

(6.10)

Taking
\xi=M
in (
6.10
) we obtain

\left|f^{(n+1)}(M)\right|\leq C\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-\frac{s-1}{j}}

for some
C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Analogously

\left|f^{(n+1)}(m)\right|\leq D\cdot\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-\frac{s-1}{j}}.

for some
D
that we may assume to be
\geq C
so that
K\leq D\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-\frac{s-1}{j}}
,
which implies
p\leq\frac{s-1}{j}
by definition of
p
. Therefore,

jp\leq s-1<1
, which proves our claim.

To prove
(xi)
, we only need to note that there always
exists an
e\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
ep<1
,
e\leq j
, so that we
can repeat the previous deduction with
e
instead of
j
.
∎

7.
Multidimensional integration and hyperlimits

In this section we want to introduce integration of GSF over functionally
compact sets with respect to an arbitrary Borel measure
\mu
.

The possibility to achieve results mirroring classical limit theorems
for this notion of integral is closely linked to the introduction
of the notion of hyperlimit, i.e. of limits of sequences of generalized
numbers
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}:\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{N}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{N}}\longrightarrow\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\sigma$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\sigma$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\sigma

and
\rho
are two gauges (see Def.
1
) and
n\to+\infty

along generalized natural numbers, i.e. for
